# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-18

## Embodied AI
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Context Matters! Social, Spatial, and Semantic Coordination with an Embodied Agent in Immersive Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21484v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI agents in Augmented Reality (AR) could enable more natural collaboration in immersive analytics by engaging users through spatial, conversational, and visual interaction. However, designing such agents requires careful attention to context. We propose a preliminary conceptual framework for context-aware design of embodied agents structured around three dimensions of spatial, social, and semantic. This framework is further categorised into embeddedness (how agents are situated in the contexts) and awareness (how agents perceive and respond to the contexts). To explore this, we present Cobot, an embodied AI agent designed as a design probe. Cobot supports context-sensitive interaction through features such as gaze cues, spatial sound, and task-aware behaviour. We reflect on its design and identify implications for future contextaware embodied agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>In 20...</summary><p>In 2025 IEEE International Symposium on Mixed and Augmented Reality Adjunct (ISMAR-Adjunct) (pp. 189-194). IEEE</p></details> |
| **[PhyMix: Towards Physically Consistent Single-Image 3D Indoor Scene Generation with Implicit--Explicit Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10125v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing methods for single-image 3D indoor scene generation often produce visually plausible results that violate real-world physical constraints, limiting their reliability in robotics, embodied AI, and virtual environment design. To bridge this gap, we introduce a Physics Evaluator that assesses four aspects (geometric priors, contact, stability, and deployability) through nine sub-constraints, establishing a benchmark for evaluating physical consistency. Our analysis reveals that even state-of-the-art methods produce scenes with substantial physical inconsistencies despite their visual plausibility. To address these limitations, we propose a framework that incorporates feedback from the Physics Evaluator into both training and inference to improve the physical plausibility of generated scenes. Specifically, PhyMix comprises two components: (i) implicit alignment via Scene-GRPO, a critic-free group-relative preference optimization strategy that uses physical evaluations to guide the generator toward physically plausible layouts, and (ii) explicit refinement via verifier-guided agentic repair, which translates evaluator diagnostics into executable scene-editing skills to correct residual violations at inference time. PhyMix unifies physical evaluation, preference-based alignment, and agentic inference-time correction to improve physical plausibility while retaining the observed scene content. Experiments show improvements in reconstruction quality and physical plausibility, while qualitative examples on stylized and real-world images illustrate the applicability of the framework beyond synthetic inputs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Plan-Bench: Can Existing VLMs Go Well Beyond Perception to Reason and Plan in Embodied Worlds?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18836v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in visual perception and spatial reasoning, leading to growing interest in using them as high-level planners for embodied AI. However, physical tasks require models to integrate spatial information, reason over temporal states, and generate multi-step plans under various constraints, raising the question of whether current VLMs can reliably support such reasoning and planning independently of execution. We introduce Plan-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating the high-level reasoning and multi-step planning capabilities of VLMs relevant to embodied worlds, while isolating the influence of action execution. It covers three complementary dimensions, spanning 16 subclasses and 699 curated samples. Extensive evaluation of 28 VLMs reveals room for improvement, with even the strongest model achieving only 75.0% accuracy. We further find that model scaling generally improves these capabilities. Ablation studies also indicate larger models show increasing potential as high-level planners. Overall, Plan-Bench provides a lightweight probe for evaluating VLMs' reasoning and planning capabilities relevant to embodied environments and assessing their potential as decision makers for physical tasks. Our benchmark and code are available at https://github.com/MinneFeng/Plan-Bench.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 11 figures, 10 tables</p></details> |
| **[OxyGen: Unified KV Cache Management for VLA Inference under Multi-Task Parallelism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14371v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI agents increasingly require parallel execution of multiple tasks, such as manipulation, conversation, and memory construction, from shared observations under distinct time constraints. Recent Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) Vision-Language-Action Models (VLAs) support such heterogeneous outputs, yet existing inference systems incur redundant computation and resource contention when serving concurrent tasks on-device. We identify isolated KV cache management as the root cause. To address this, we propose unified KV cache management, an inference design that treats the KV cache as a first-class shared resource across tasks and control cycles. This abstraction enables two key optimizations: cross-task KV sharing eliminates redundant prefill of shared observations, while cross-frame continuous batching batches unfinished language requests from different frames and advances them within a per-frame decoding budget. We implement this design in OxyGen and evaluate $π_{0.5}$ on NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4090 and Jetson AGX Thor. OxyGen achieves up to 3.7$\times$ speedup over isolated execution, delivering over 200 tokens/s language throughput and 70 Hz action frequency simultaneously on RTX 4090. We also validate the design on Xiaomi-Robotics-0 and StarVLA across different backbones and expert configurations. We further demonstrate the inference gains on a humanoid robot with on-board Jetson AGX Thor.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[Thinker-WM: A Unified World Model with Shared Multimodal Representations for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18447v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models have emerged as a promising paradigm for embodied AI, yet existing systems often remain fragmented in the modalities they model and the functions they support. In particular, visual dynamics, geometric structure, and executable action are commonly coupled through separate interfaces or downstream modules rather than being jointly represented and evolved within a shared dynamics model. In this work, we present Thinker-WM, a unified multimodal world model that jointly models and generates RGB, metric depth, and robot action through a shared intermediate Unified Dynamics Transformer with modality-specific encoders and decoders. The model performs multimodal unification at the representation level: appearance, geometry, and action are transformed into a common dynamic representation and jointly evolved by shared parameters, while their distinct output structures are preserved. The same parameterization supports multiple functions, including joint video-action generation, world prediction, policy inference, action-conditioned generation, video-to-action generation, and inverse dynamics. To examine whether the shared representation captures action-related information, we introduce a lightweight motion probe that shows the frozen visual representation contains recoverable motion information. Thinker-WM is trained on a heterogeneous corpus comprising approximately 20K hours of egocentric video, 10K hours of real-robot data, and 1K hours of simulation data, including approximately 4K hours of real-robot data with aligned depth supervision. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong performance across embodied world-model evaluations. Thinker-WM ranks first on EWMBench, WorldOlympiad and WorldArena, while controlled studies further demonstrate the benefits of action conditioning, geometric interaction, and multi-function training within the shared representation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://thinkercosmos.ubtrobot.com/thinker-wm</p></details> |
| **[Multi-View Relational Distillation for Spatial Reasoning with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10864v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved strong image and video understanding, yet their visual-spatial representations remain geometrically fragile, leading to failures in spatial reasoning needed for embodied AI, robotics, and autonomous driving. Prior approaches to geometry grounding either fine-tune VLMs on spatial question answering, which can perpetuate spurious visual representations, or fuse features from large geometry-grounded vision models, which substantially increases model size at inference. Knowledge distillation from geometry-grounded vision models offers an alternative, but directly matching multi-view teacher features can disrupt the pretrained alignment between visual and textual representations, degrading object- and language-semantic capabilities. We propose multi-view relational distillation (MVRD), which distills patch-wise cosine similarities across views instead of the teacher features themselves. These relations encode geometric correspondences adequate for spatial understanding, while leaving the student representation underdetermined, allowing it to remain close to its pretrained vision- language space. Across representative VLMs, MVRD improves visual-spatial reasoning, outperforming supervised fine-tuning and feature distillation while approaching feature fusion methods with considerably fewer added parameters and lower latency. We show that MVRD makes visual representations more geometric while retaining language alignment, and generalizes to 3D scene understanding tasks such as object grounding, dense captioning, and question answering.</p></details> |  |
| **[HelpMeOut: A Neuropsychology-Grounded Benchmark for Error-Aware Monitoring of Household Task Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17694v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce HelpMeOut, an embodied AI benchmark for error-aware monitoring of household task execution. Existing embodied household benchmarks typically assume that humans execute tasks correctly, but this assumption does not hold for cognitively impaired persons or older adults whose everyday activities produce structured execution errors. To address this gap, HelpMeOut operationalizes research on everyday action errors into 20000 paired activity episodes, covering nine neuropsychologically motivated error categories. In each episode, agents observe a streaming human activity trajectory and must monitor whether the execution is unfolding correctly, diagnosing any structured errors as early as possible. We evaluate learned-monitor, symbolic, change-point, and frontier LLM prompting baselines. Results show that these methods perform poorly as error monitors, often missing when execution goes wrong. These results suggest a gap left by standard goal-inference and task-completion benchmarks, making HelpMeOut a testbed for error-aware assistive agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 10th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL 2026). 30 pages (9-page main text), 8 figures, 18 tables. Project page: https://whrcn.github.io/HelpMeOut-Official/</p></details> |
| **[ParallelWorld: Test-Time Scaling for Agentic Embodied Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22971v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI has emerged as a key paradigm for autonomous systems operating in complex physical environments. While end-to-end models have established strong local capabilities for perception and control, scaling these capabilities to extended, multi-stage tasks remains difficult. Agentic embodied systems address this challenge by introducing high-level planners that organize perception, reasoning, and executable skills over longer interaction horizons. However, existing agentic embodied frameworks typically generate actions for direct execution without explicitly simulating how alternative action sequences shape future observations and task progress, leading agents to commit to suboptimal interactions before considering their long-term consequences. To address this limitation, we propose ParallelWorld, an adaptive-horizon test-time scaling framework that grounds agentic embodied reasoning in the prospective simulation of action outcomes. Specifically, we design an agent system to simulate and evaluate alternative trajectories in parallel before committing to an action. A verifier assesses intermediate state transitions with respect to the task objective, dynamically pruning unpromising branches and guiding subsequent expansion. Based on the evaluated long-horizon outcomes, the agent selects a trajectory, executes only its first action, and replans as new observations become available. Experiments on ESI-Bench and LIBERO-Pro demonstrate improvements in active spatial reasoning and language-conditioned manipulation, respectively, highlighting the effectiveness of prospective planning for both understanding and manipulation tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://agenticei.github.io/ParallelWorld-page/ Code: https://github.com/AgenticEI/ParallelWorld</p></details> |
| **[Vision-Predictor-Action: Towards Efficient Embodied AI via Neuro-Symbolic JEPA Predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15556v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models commonly generate robot actions token by token through Large Language Model (LLM) decoders, causing inference latency to grow with action sequence length and limiting closed-loop control frequency. We present Goal-Conditioned Vision-Predictor-Action (VPA), which replaces autoregressive decoders with a lightweight latent control loop. A shared vision encoder and a frozen text encoder embed observations, goal images, and instructions; a neuro-symbolic selector chooses a discrete skill; a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) predictor forecasts the next latent state; and a flow-matching solver generates an entire action chunk in a few integration steps, so that sequential network evaluations per decision step do not depend on the action horizon. We analyze inference complexity, regularization against representation collapse, and conditions under which latent sub-goal thresholding limits error accumulation across task phases. As an initial proof of concept, we evaluated VPA on the LIBERO-10 suite. The trainable model has under 20M parameters (checkpoint under 75 MB, excluding a frozen 63.4M-parameter CLIP text encoder); a decision step of a single-camera reference configuration takes 10-14 ms on an Apple M4; and trained only on LIBERO-10 demonstrations in under three hours on a single GPU, a two-camera policy succeeded in 40.0% of 200 evaluation episodes. These preliminary results from a single training run suggest the approach is workable. Code and weights are available at https://github.com/ylee-sling/Vision-Predictor-Action and https://huggingface.co/ylee-sling/vpa-libero10, and archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22960920.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables. Code: https://github.com/ylee-sling/Vision-Predictor-Action | Checkpoints: https://huggingface.co/ylee-sling/vpa-libero10 | Zenodo: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22960920</p></details> |
| **[LERR-Nav: Learning to Coordinate Exploration, Recovery, and Reminiscing for Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15362v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Object Navigation in unseen environments remains a fundamental challenge in embodied AI, as it requires agents to generalize visual and spatial reasoning to novel layouts. While recent advances in large language model (LLM) and vision-language model (VLM) have enhanced semantic mapping, spatial memory, and waypoint selecting for navigation, successful navigation requires more than selecting the next promising waypoint. A robot must also recognize stalled progress, escape unproductive behaviors, and revisit overlooked target cues. Addressing these challenges requires both specialized navigation strategies and a high-level policy that coordinates their execution. Motivated by the analysis of failed navigation episodes, we propose LERR-Nav, an object navigation framework that coordinates exploration, recovery, and reminiscing through a learned high-level policy. The framework comprises four complementary navigation strategies: value-function-guided exploration, LLM-guided exploration, recovery from stalled or repetitive behavior, and memory-guided reminiscing. To support history-aware coordination, we maintain the execution memory that preserves textual summaries of all strategy executions alongside a sliding window of map sequences. To learn the coordination policy, we initialize a high-level vision-language agent from Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct and train it through a two-stage curriculum. The first stage uses map-centric supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to establish spatial grounding. The second stage performs SFT on variable-length navigation segments to learn which navigation strategy to invoke next based on current maps and the hybrid execution memory. Experiments on HM3D and MP3D demonstrate state-of-the-art navigation performance, while ablation studies validate the contributions of individual components.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond LLM Serving: Characterizing Vision-Language-Action Workloads for Embodied AI System Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05062v2)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) models translate multimodal observations into low-level robot actions. During robot operation, each control period sets an inference deadline, and overruns leave the robot acting on stale observations, reducing task success. Meeting this deadline motivates on-device or nearby edge execution, where a single robot requires batch-1 inference outside the design point of LLM serving systems. Although VLA architectures combine familiar vision-language, autoregressive, and diffusion-style components, their runtime behavior in this batch-1 control setting remains uncharacterized. We characterize four representative VLA models on an edge GPU server and two onboard SoCs, using single-inference profiling and 43,200 closed-loop episodes. Action tensor dimensionality determines whether a stage is memory- or compute-bound, platform balance can shift that bottleneck, and GPU frequency scaling yields a platform-dependent energy-latency sweet spot. In closed-loop operation, overlapping inference with action execution creates an accuracy-speed-energy tradeoff, and no configuration is Pareto-dominant across deployment SLOs. These results guide joint design of VLA model architectures, hardware, and runtime policies.</p></details> | <details><summary>To ap...</summary><p>To appear in the Proceedings of the 32nd ACM International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems (ASPLOS '27). 17 pages, 14 figures</p></details> |
| **[LIVIN: Benchmarking Spatial and Embodied Intelligence in Digital Twins of Lived-In Homes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12069v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realistic household simulation must capture not only diverse environments but also the lived-in object arrangements and spatial constraints that shape robot motion and interaction. Existing resources often trade off scale, real-world correspondence, and interaction readiness, leaving a gap in faithful, interactive replicas of how real homes are actually arranged. To this end, we introduce LIVIN, a benchmark for spatial and embodied intelligence built on digital twins of 30 diverse lived-in homes. These replicas preserve observed room layouts, furniture configurations, and everyday belongings. To construct them, we design a human-in-the-loop workflow comprising instance recognition, architectural reconstruction, and object generation and placement, with intermediate results reviewed and corrected by humans against the source observations at each stage. We evaluate four tasks in LIVIN: 3D detection, 3D reconstruction, navigation, and loco-manipulation. Our evaluations show that current methods remain challenged by the dense object arrangements, occlusions, limited free space, and constrained interaction regions found in realistic lived-in homes. We hope LIVIN will help advance embodied AI in real-world homes, from spatial understanding to robotic interaction, and ultimately bring embodied intelligence into everyday home environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[USER: A Unified and Extensible System for Real-World Online Policy Learning in Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07837v5)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online policy learning directly in the physical world is a promising yet challenging direction for embodied intelligence. Unlike simulation, real-world systems cannot be arbitrarily accelerated, cheaply reset, or massively replicated, suggesting that real-world policy learning is not merely an algorithmic problem, but inherently a systems problem. We present USER, a \underline{U}nified and extensible \underline{S}yst\underline{E}m for real-world online policy lea\underline{R}ning. On the systems side, USER introduces a hardware abstraction layer for unified robot management and an adaptive communication plane that enables efficient cloud-edge training. On the learning side, USER adopts a fully asynchronous training framework, designs a persistent and cache-aware replay buffer, and provides extensible abstractions for rewards, algorithms, and policies. Experiments in both simulation and the real world demonstrate that USER supports multi-robot coordination, heterogeneous manipulators, cloud-edge training with large models, and long-running asynchronous training. Together, these capabilities establish USER as a unified and extensible systems foundation for real-world online policy learning.</p></details> | Accepted to RSS 2026 |
| **[OmniDex: Scaling Dexterous Hand Grasping to Diverse Cluttered Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11194v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dexterous grasping is the foundational primitive in embodied AI, demanding massive data to train robust models. As real-world data collection is expensive, simulation has become the mainstream paradigm. Yet, while cluttered scenes best reflect real-world applications, learning to grasp within them is bottlenecked by a critical scarcity of large-scale data. To resolve this, we curate high-quality 3D objects and supporting bases, proposing a scalable seed-and-filter strategy that bypasses sluggish scene-level optimization. This yields an unprecedented benchmark comprising over 2.6 million scenes and 0.4B scene-specific grasp ground truths, featuring diverse realistic layouts paired with rich semantic and geometric observations. Furthermore, we introduce the OmniDex model to overcome the grasp multimodality and last-millimeter precision errors plaguing current generative models. By coupling Soft Winner-Takes-All learning with human-inspired physical constraints during training, and utilizing physics-driven ranking, our approach achieves robust dexterous grasping without the latency of post-optimization. Experimental results show that OmniDex model achieves state-of-the-art performance and strong generalization across diverse scenes, views, and unseen objects.</p></details> |  |
| **[Generative AI for Autonomous Driving: Frontiers and Opportunities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08854v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) constitutes a transformative technological wave that reconfigures industries through its unparalleled capabilities for content creation, reasoning, planning, and multimodal understanding. This revolutionary force offers the most promising path yet toward solving one of engineering's grandest challenges: achieving reliable, fully autonomous driving, particularly the pursuit of Level 5 autonomy. This survey delivers a comprehensive and critical synthesis of the emerging role of GenAI across the autonomous driving stack. We begin by distilling the principles and trade-offs of modern generative modeling, encompassing VAEs, GANs, Diffusion Models, and Large Language Models (LLMs). We then map their frontier applications in image, LiDAR, trajectory, occupancy, video generation as well as LLM-guided reasoning and decision making. We categorize practical applications, such as synthetic data workflows, end-to-end driving strategies, high-fidelity digital twin systems, smart transportation networks, and cross-domain transfer to embodied AI. We identify key obstacles and possibilities such as comprehensive generalization across rare cases, evaluation and safety checks, budget-limited implementation, regulatory compliance, ethical concerns, and environmental effects, while proposing research plans across theoretical assurances, trust metrics, transport integration, and socio-technical influence. By unifying these threads, the survey provides a forward-looking reference for researchers, engineers, and policymakers navigating the convergence of generative AI and advanced autonomous mobility. An actively maintained repository of cited works is available at https://github.com/taco-group/GenAI4AD.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ACM Computer Survey</p></details> |
| **[World Models' Last Exam in Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08791v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video world models can produce visually convincing yet physically inconsistent sequences, raising concerns about their reliability for prediction and planning in embodied AI systems. Existing evaluations often rely on model-based judgments or reference videos, while direct physical tests largely focus on mechanics. We introduce World Models' Last Exam in Physics, a measurement-based benchmark for evaluating physical consistency in video world models. The benchmark comprises 40 controlled tasks spanning mechanics, optics, fluids, thermal and phase-change phenomena, electromagnetism, and surface tension. Each task pairs an initial image and a generation prompt with predefined physical criteria, enabling interpretable tests of observable physical relationships without requiring reference videos. Its evaluator combines task-observability screening with task-specific quantitative physical measurements. Experiments on eight video generation models across 1,280 videos reveal persistent physical inconsistencies and substantial variation across tasks, with the best model achieving an overall score of 57.76 out of 100. Evaluation on synthetic videos with known physical relationships provides evidence for the validity of the measurement module under controlled conditions. The evaluator also achieves higher agreement with human judgments than a direct vision-language model baseline in both within-task rankings and pairwise comparisons. By combining coverage across physical domains with scores grounded in measurable evidence and explicit measurement limitations, the benchmark provides an interpretable basis for diagnosing physical inconsistencies and tracking progress toward physically consistent video world models.</p></details> |  |
| **[WorldSolver: Can LLM Agents Simulate the Physical Dynamics via Solver Generation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08720v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based agents are increasingly advancing scientific and engineering problem solving, with physics simulation emerging as a challenging yet practical testbed for reproducing complex physical phenomena with application in embodied AI, games and films. As the workhorse of such simulation, a solver computes how the state of a dynamic system evolves over time. Building such solvers requires physical understanding to identify appropriate models, mathematical reasoning to formulate the underlying dynamics, and software engineering to implement them as executable code, yet this capability of LLM agents remains underexplored. To this end, we introduce WorldSolver, a benchmark of 168 simulation tasks derived from physical phenomena in 61 classic computer graphics papers, spanning 7 physical domains. Each task contains a code scaffold that provides a fixed simulation environment for the scene, with the solver implementation left for the agent to complete. Specifically, we evaluate them along three dimensions: Execution Checks for successful execution, Visual Fidelity for reproducing the intended dynamic behavior in the rendered simulation, and Physical Plausibility for physics-grounded verification of the generated dynamics. Experiments on frontier agents reveal that producing executable solvers is difficult itself, and satisfying visual and physical correctness is even harder. GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude-Opus-5 perform comparatively better than the other evaluated agents, yet achieve overall scores of only 48.7% and 46.7%, respectively. WorldSolver is an early step toward agentic solver generation, and we hope it helps drive progress toward agents that can faithfully simulate the dynamic physical world. Code is available at https://github.com/sirujiang/WorldSolver.</p></details> |  |
| **[Is this machine playing?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07130v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We placed a modern AI coding assistant in an unintended role: as the mind of a body on an unknown digital island. With only a minimal instruction mentioning no specific task, reward, or activity, the machine started animating its virtual body. Across thirty-hour runs, the embodied AI agent climbed hills, stacked blocks into towers, drew mandalas, reinterpreted sports, ran experiments on the physics of its world, and learned techniques that later expanded what it could accomplish. These activities recurred across thirteen agents but diverged into distinct histories. We examine whether this behavior satisfies classical criteria for play and ask whether play can become a mode of machine development.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages of main text, 17 figures</p></details> |
| **[Look-Before-Move: Narrative-Grounded World Visual Attention in Dynamic 3D Story Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26964v3)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As embodied AI and world models increasingly operate in dynamic 3D environments, visual perception must move beyond passively interpreting given observations toward actively deciding what to observe. We study this problem through camera planning in dynamic 3D story worlds, where the camera must not only generate smooth motion, but also decide what visual evidence should be acquired before it moves. We formulate this capability as Narrative-Grounded World Visual Attention, where the camera acts as an embodied observer that determines what to observe, how to compose the observation, and how to shift attention over time under narrative intent and physical 3D constraints. To realize this capability, we propose Look-Before-Move, a camera planning framework that separates observation specification from motion execution. It first builds a Semantic Observation Contract to convert directorial intent into executable visual constraints, then performs Monte Carlo Viewpoint Search to find narrative-compliant and geometrically feasible viewpoints, and finally applies Semantic Trajectory Grounding to connect selected viewpoints into continuous, collision-aware, and temporally coherent camera motion. We further construct a dynamic 3D Story World Benchmark based on StoryBlender, covering 50 stories, 457 scenes, and 1585 shots with animated characters, semantic scene configurations, and executable 3D environments. Experiments show that our framework improves subject perception, intent consistency, and trajectory quality over representative baselines, demonstrating the importance of organizing visual attention before generating camera motion.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Main Track, Poster). 30 pages (including references and appendices), 19 figures</p></details> |
| **[Inspect Robots: Evaluating the Capabilities and Safety of Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06306v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General purpose language models are increasingly able to control robotic hardware. Understanding the capabilities and safety of these models when embodied is therefore increasingly important for understanding their societal impact and risks. To this end, we introduce Inspect Robots, a modular, open-source framework for developing and running evaluations of embodied agents. Inspect Robots pairs customizable, reusable abstractions for specifying physical evaluations and analyzing their results with infrastructure that automates evaluation setup, execution and termination. We demonstrate Inspect Robots by using it to evaluate the capabilities and safety of six policies based on frontier language models. Inspect Robots has seen significant early uptake, receiving nearly 100,000 downloads in the three months since its release.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to The Science of Physical AI Safety (SPAIS) Workshop at CoRL 2026</p></details> |
| **[A Survey of Robotic Navigation and Manipulation with Physics Simulators in the Era of Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01458v3)** | 2026-10-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Navigation and manipulation are core capabilities in Embodied AI, but training agents to perform them directly in the real world is costly, time-consuming, and unsafe. Therefore, sim-to-real transfer has emerged as a key approach, yet the sim-to-real gap persists. This survey examines how physics simulators address this gap by analyzing properties that have received limited attention in prior surveys. We also analyze their features for navigation and manipulation tasks, as well as their hardware requirements. Additionally, we offer a resource with benchmark datasets, metrics, simulation platforms, and methods to help researchers select suitable tools while accounting for hardware constraints.</p></details> | <details><summary>35 pa...</summary><p>35 pages, 9 figures. Accepted to ACM Computing Surveys (CSUR)</p></details> |
| **[EnvDreamer: Large-Scale Multimodal-to-Environment Generation for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04301v1)** | 2026-10-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large datasets and high capacity models have accelerated progress in vision and language. This work introduces a platform aimed at bringing comparable gains to embodied learning, world models, and robotics. We present EnvDreamer, a framework that uses large language and vision language models to generate Unreal Engine 5 environments for embodied AI and robot training. EnvDreamer enables sampling of large, diverse, interactive, customizable, and validator passed virtual environments for training and evaluation across navigation, interaction, and manipulation. We illustrate the platform with a large set of generated scenes and simple baselines. Policies trained on EnvDreamer generated environments, without explicit mapping or human task supervision, achieve competitive results on multiple embodied benchmarks spanning navigation, rearrangement, and manipulation. EnvDreamer also supports image-conditioned reconstruction for real-to-sim studies. Finally, we release EnvDreamer-20k, a dataset of 20,000 validator passed environments with task programs, scene graphs, trajectories, and metadata to support reproducible benchmarking.</p></details> |  |
| **[From bits to atoms: A survey of cross-layer safety in Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14038v1)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied Artificial Intelligence (AI) introduces cyber-physical risks because digital failures and attacks can cause physical harm. This survey organizes embodied AI safety through a four-layer reference architecture -- comprising the physical interaction, system and middleware, algorithm and decision, and cloud-edge and data layers -- and uses it to connect vulnerabilities, propagation paths, and safeguards across the system stack. It traces the evolution of safety paradigms and examines how faults and attacks cross architectural boundaries to produce physical consequences. Furthermore, the survey systematizes composite attack chains, outlines multi-layered defense strategies, and reviews representative embodied safety benchmarks. Ultimately, we advocate for unifying software alignment with rigorous hardware constraints to build trustworthy systems capable of operating safely within human-centric environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Revie...</summary><p>Review article published in AI Plus. 25 pages, 6 figures. | **[A Token Service Interface for AI-Native RANs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02618v1)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative and embodied AI services exchange token streams within continuing inference and control loops. | **[Valerant: An Automatic Navigable Game Map Generator via Action-Conditioned World Model Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09418v3)** | 2026-09-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World Action Models (WAMs) couple predictive world modeling with action generation, allowing anticipated future states to guide agent behavior. | **[When Robots Mishear Us: Mapping the Safety Risks of Voice-Controlled Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28518v1)** | 2026-08-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We investigate whether automatic speech recognition (ASR) errors in user input can lead to unsafe outputs from Embodied AI (EAI) models. | **[Value as Memory: Online Value-Guided Post-training for General Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22368v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world post-training of generalist robot policies faces two challenges: partial observability makes action selection ambiguous, while offline post-training methods delay feedback from deployment experience. We present Value as Memory (VaM), a framework that addresses these challenges through value caching and online real-world reinforcement learning (RL). Value caching conditions actions on recent value predictions as task-progress memory, while online updates enable timely policy improvement by combining offline demonstrations, off-policy interventions, and on-policy rollouts for sample-efficient adaptation. As a result, VaM transitions from offline RL warmup to online autonomous learning with sparse rewards. Across long-horizon tasks involving deformable objects and contact-rich manipulation, VaM yields a mean absolute success-rate gain of $29.4\%$ with value caching, while achieving a $32.8\%$ gain with online autonomous learning over matched offline training. The evaluated online schedule performs $8\times$ as many policy iterations under the same gradient-step budget, with more than $2\times$ lower recorded time per gradient step with concurrent data collection and training.</p></details> |  |
| **[GRASP: Plan-Guided Graph Retrieval with Adaptive Fusion and Reranking on Semi-Structured Knowledge Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30237v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval over semi-structured knowledge bases (SKBs) must handle both textual descriptions and relational constraints. A small LLM planner translates the query into an executable graph plan in a single call, its graph ranking is fused with a text ranking, and a fine-tuned reranker reorders the fused candidates. Instead of training the planner to be accurate on its own, we optimize it with reinforcement learning to maximize the gain its graph ranking brings to the fused ranking. On three STaRK benchmarks, GRASP achieves the best results on all metrics, improving the average Hit@1 by 18.6 points over the strongest baseline, while taking only 2.47 seconds per query.</p></details> |  |

Under regularized importance sampling, the importance-weighted policy gradient is no longer invariant to constant shifts in the reward. This note develops this covariance perspective for both sequence-level and token-level importance sampling. Whether both forms of centering should be used simultaneously depends on whether importance sampling is applied at the sequence level or at the token level. | **[RM-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Generative Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20174v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning on Mixed-domain preference data (Mix-RL) has become the mainstream approach for training general-purpose Generative Reward Models (GRMs). | **[LIBERO-PeRM: Benchmarking Personalized Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22372v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personalization is essential for general-purpose robots to transition into household environments and achieve true practicality. While general robotic manipulation has been extensively studied, personalized robotic manipulation (PeRM) lacks a controlled benchmark for separating task completion from preference satisfaction. We introduce LIBERO-PeRM, a benchmark for preference learning in embodied agents. LIBERO-PeRM studies five questions: 1) how policies follow preferences in zero-shot settings; 2) how they learn single preferences from demonstrations; 3) how efficiently they acquire preferences as demonstrations increase; 4) how learned preferences generalize across layouts and tasks; and 5) how policies compose preferences and behave under conflicts. We propose a controlled formulation and taxonomy of observable preferences in simulation, covering motion, skill, and user-profile choices. This formulation is restricted to language-expressible preferences that can be instantiated in LIBERO without changing the task goal, evaluated automatically, and reused across tasks. Building on LIBERO, our procedural pipeline generates paired demonstrations with and without preferences. We create five task suites (312 tasks) with high-quality demonstrations, rich metadata, and preference-satisfaction metrics. Experiments show that current VLA policies struggle with zero-shot personalization, while SFT makes preferences learnable. Mixed training achieves better no-preference retention than sequential adaptation and improves performance with contextual prompts compared with preference-only training, but can weaken motion-preference learning; preference generalization, decomposition, and conflict handling remain difficult. The code, datasets, and demos can be found on the project website: https://libero-perm.github.io/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Confe...</summary><p>Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026); Project Website: https://libero-perm.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Value as Memory: Online Value-Guided Post-training for General Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22368v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world post-training of generalist robot policies faces two challenges: partial observability makes action selection ambiguous, while offline post-training methods delay feedback from deployment experience. We present Value as Memory (VaM), a framework that addresses these challenges through value caching and online real-world reinforcement learning (RL). Value caching conditions actions on recent value predictions as task-progress memory, while online updates enable timely policy improvement by combining offline demonstrations, off-policy interventions, and on-policy rollouts for sample-efficient adaptation. As a result, VaM transitions from offline RL warmup to online autonomous learning with sparse rewards. Across long-horizon tasks involving deformable objects and contact-rich manipulation, VaM yields a mean absolute success-rate gain of $29.4\%$ with value caching, while achieving a $32.8\%$ gain with online autonomous learning over matched offline training. The evaluated online schedule performs $8\times$ as many policy iterations under the same gradient-step budget, with more than $2\times$ lower recorded time per gradient step with concurrent data collection and training.</p></details> |  |
| **[T2-z: Test-Time Optimization of Environment Latents for Robotic World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22364v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting how the world responds to an action requires more than what can be seen: robots must account for environmental factors that are neither directly observable nor explicitly specified, from physical properties such as friction and mass to human-defined environmental rules. We introduce T2-Z for test-time optimization of environment latents, which augments a robotic world model with a learnable environment latent to condition world predictions. This compact latent captures underlying environmental variation without supervision of what environmental factors exist or what values they take. During training, T2-Z alternates between two stages: a world-model optimization stage that fixes the latents and learns general action-conditioned dynamics across environments; and an environment-latent optimization stage that freezes the world model and optimizes the conditioning latents, allowing latent structure to emerge purely through world prediction. At test time, the world model remains frozen and a newly initialized environment latent is optimized from one or more test-time interactions, adapting the model to previously unseen environmental configurations. Across 7 simulated settings and 4 real-world robotic tasks, T2-Z reduces object prediction error metric from 15.20 to 7.00 and raises action success from 33.73% to 72.96%. Its emergent latents capture structured representations of individual and compounded factors across diverse visual scenes. Ablations further show that T2-Z can efficiently infer environment latents from as little as a single test-time interaction trajectory.</p></details> |  |
| **[Spark-0: Scaling Spatially Aligned Tactile Pretraining for Robot Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22342v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalist robot policies benefit from large-scale visual pretraining but often struggle with contact-rich dexterous manipulation, where force, compliance, and incipient slip are hard to observe from images. We present Spark-0, a spatially aligned vision-tactile-language-action (VTLA) model that uses touch in two complementary ways. During pretraining, image-space contact maps derived from tactile gloves, together with features from a frozen 3D geometry teacher, supervise the visual tokens; these objectives are removed at inference. In parallel, a tactile pathway encodes regional force histories and 3D taxel states to condition the action expert. A single checkpoint therefore supports both vision-only and tactile-conditioned deployment. To scale this supervision, we build HumanTouch, a 1,000-hour dataset of full-hand tactile human manipulation, and pretrain Spark-0 on it together with egocentric video, robot data, and simulation. We also release SparkArena, which pairs vision-only simulated tasks with tactile-conditioned real-robot tasks on one dexterous embodiment. Spark-0 achieves the highest mean success rate on both SparkArena tracks and ranks first on all eight aggregate metrics of the Ego Humanoid Manipulation Benchmark. Compared with vision-only pretraining on the same data and compute with touch discarded, retaining the touch of 1,000 hours of HumanTouch raises vision-only success on Ego Humanoid from 52.5% to 71.7%, and success on SparkArena-Real, where touch is also used at deployment, from 44.0% to 52.0%, indicating that touch serves not only as a policy input but also as scalable supervision for dexterous visuomotor pretraining. The Website is available at: https://xsparkai.com/Spark-0.</p></details> | <details><summary>Websi...</summary><p>Website: https://xsparkai.com/Spark-0</p></details> |
| **[Sim-to-Real Reinforcement Learning for Path-Safe Target Reaching in Robotic Harvesting of Hydroponic Lettuce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22337v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic harvesting requires precise manipulation to ensure accurate positioning of the end-effector, especially in a constraint workspace. This study introduces the Path-Safe Approach Proximal Policy Optimization (PSA-PPO), a target-conditioned local reaching framework designed for robotic harvesting of nutrient film technique (NFT)-hydroponic lettuce in greenhouses. The proposed PSA-PPO preserves the standard clipped PPO objective while introducing a harvesting-oriented policy formulation that integrates target-conditioned observations, corridor-aware spatial constraints, trajectory-history-based safety encoding, and task-specific success criteria for constrained robotic manipulation. PSAPPO was trained entirely in Isaac Lab and successfully transferred to a physical UR10e robotic arm without any real-world policy fine-tuning. In a controlled physical benchmark against 150 scheduled targets, PSA-PPO achieved the highest reaching rate of 94.7%, outperforming Cartesian+TOTG (86.0%), RRTConnect (88.0%), and RRT* (80.0%), while reducing median RMS jerk by 39.8 to 73.0% relative to the classical planners during the final approach segment. A formulation ablation evaluated over 200 simulation targets demonstrates that path-safety and side-corridor shaping are the principal drivers of harvesting-compatible performance: removing either term substantially degrades strict success even when terminal endpoint accuracy is preserved or improved, confirming that optimization toward the endpoint alone is an insufficient objective for constrained crop-proximal manipulation. Finally, PSA-PPO was integrated with an RGB-D vision module and an end-effector and evaluated as a complete greenhouse harvesting system across 144 lettuce harvesting attempts, achieving 94.4% vision-guided target reaching and 86.1% complete harvesting success.</p></details> |  |
| **[Mem4Act: Learning Effective Latent Memory for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22323v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon robotic manipulation fundamentally requires memory: agents must retain task-relevant information from past observations and interactions and use it to guide future actions. Existing approaches typically preserve raw historical observations, compressed visual tokens, or language summaries, but these representations either expose the action policy to increasingly long histories or risk discarding task-relevant temporal, semantic, and perceptual information. We introduce Mem4Act, a framework for learning effective latent memory as a fixed-size representation explicitly optimized for long-horizon robotic control. Mem4Act addresses three coupled challenges: how to efficiently cover long histories, what information memory should retain, and how to ensure the policy actually relies on memory. To this end, it organizes history via multi-granularity temporal aggregation, preserves complementary semantic and perceptual information through question-answering supervision and key-frame DINO feature reconstruction, and employs memory-first two-stage training to encourage greater use of memory by the action policy. The resulting memory captures both high-level task state and fine-grained perceptual evidence while providing a bounded interface to the action policy. Across RoboMME, RMBench, and four real-world manipulation tasks, Mem4Act achieves state-of-the-art performance on memory-dependent control, with success rates of 49.88%, 61.0%, and 81.5%, respectively. Extensive ablations further demonstrate the importance of temporal aggregation, complementary memory supervision, and memory-first training.</p></details> |  |
| **[Bi-Level Optimization for Contact and Motion Planning in Rope-Assisted Legged Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26910v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a planning pipeline framework for locomotion in rope-assisted robots climbing vertical surfaces. The proposed framework is formulated as a bi-level optimization scheme that addresses a mixed-integer problem: selecting feasible terrain regions for landing while simultaneously optimizing the control inputs, namely rope tensions and leg forces, and landing location. The outer level of the optimization is solved using the Cross-Entropy Method, while the inner level relies on gradient-based nonlinear optimization to compute dynamically feasible motions. The approach is validated on a novel climbing robot platform, ALPINE, across a variety of challenging terrain configurations.</p></details> |  |
| **[RMBench: Memory-Dependent Robotic Manipulation Benchmark with Insights into Policy Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01229v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic manipulation policies have made rapid progress in recent years, yet most existing approaches give limited consideration to memory capabilities. Consequently, they struggle to solve tasks that require reasoning over historical observations and maintaining task-relevant information over time, which are common requirements in real-world manipulation scenarios. Although several memory-aware policies have been proposed, systematic evaluation of memory-dependent manipulation remains underexplored, and the relationship between architectural design choices and memory performance is still not well understood. To address this gap, we introduce RMBench, a simulation benchmark comprising 9 manipulation tasks that span multiple levels of memory complexity, enabling systematic evaluation of policy memory capabilities. We further propose Mem-0, a modular manipulation policy with explicit memory components designed to support controlled ablation studies. Through extensive simulation and real-world experiments, we identify memory-related limitations in existing policies and provide empirical insights into how architectural design choices influence memory performance. The website is available at https://rmbench.github.io/.</p></details> | <details><summary>websi...</summary><p>website: https://rmbench.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[MOSS: Mode-Oriented Source Shaping for Robot Flow Matching Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22193v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In robotic manipulation, the same observation often admits multiple feasible actions. Generative policies such as diffusion and flow matching naturally capture such multimodal behaviors, yet which mode is executed is left to random sampling and cannot be actively controlled. We propose Mode-Oriented Source Shaping (MOSS), which turns mode selection into an explicit choice of where generation starts. We first reveal, both empirically and theoretically, that deterministic flow transport induces mode-dependent structure in the source space. Under ideal deterministic ODE transport, distinct behavioral modes have distinct source-space preimages, while practical approximation errors can blur their boundaries due to imbalanced data, limited model capacity, and few inference steps. Motivated by this source-space structure, MOSS constructs semantically aligned mode-specific Gaussian sources that encode mode identity in the initial noise. A modality-strength estimator predicts the local degree of multimodality, which adaptively controls the geometry of the mode-specific sources. It contracts them toward a shared Gaussian where behaviors are similar and separates them near behavioral branching points. During deployment, the desired behavior is selected at inference simply by sampling from the corresponding component. Across 6 simulated and 3 real-world manipulation tasks, MOSS steers to the target mode up to 100% of the time with high task success, recovers minority modes at over 90% guidance rate under a 10:90 imbalance dataset, and surpasses mode-conditioned flow policy by 43.3 points of guidance rate on real robots.</p></details> |  |
| **[CBF-Based Control Allocation for Omnidirectional Tiltrotor Aerial Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22057v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Actively tilting aerial robots can generate six-dimensional wrenches, but slow servo response can make a desired wrench infeasible during reconfiguration. This paper develops a control barrier function (CBF) allocator that preserves wrench feasibility under bounded servo-configuration mismatch. An exact equivalent-wrench identity connects the thrust required at nominal and actual configurations. For a nonsingular six-channel allocation map, inverse allocation reduces robust wrench containment to twelve thrust margins tightened by an ellipsoidal reserve. Their complete derivatives yield a six-variable servo-rate quadratic program, followed by differential thrust allocation for tracking. A conditional forward-invariance result establishes actual-configuration feasibility under reserve coverage, feasible initialization, and admissible CBF inputs. A complementary MuJoCo study evaluates a measured-angle implementation against geometric, augmented differential, and normalized differential allocation in circular and cartwheel maneuvers, with and without disturbances. The proposed implementation achieves lower position error in all four recorded scenarios, with an attitude tradeoff in circular tracking. During the disturbed cartwheel, the desired wrench is feasible at all 1751 stored samples of continuous rotation; baseline violation rates range from 60.8% to 100%. These comparisons evaluate sampled feasibility; validation of the nominal-trajectory certificate requires nominal-state and reserve-coverage records.</p></details> | 11 pages |
| **[De-Risking Robotic Welding for In-Space Metal Joining Through Physics-Grounded Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21939v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Growing interest in in-space servicing, manufacturing, and assembly has renewed attention on reliable metal joining for modular construction and on-orbit repair. Robotic welding is promising, but reduced-gravity and low-pressure operation remains poorly characterised and flight-like tests are limited. The UK Space Agency-supported ISPARK project is developing a physics-grounded digital twin for structured process de-risking. This paper evaluates a model-based prototype combining a finite-volume thermal weld-pool model, Gaussian-process (GP) and neural-network surrogates, and a simulated six-axis manipulator. The reduced model includes solid-liquid phase change, effective thermocapillary transport, and a local-pressure parameter. Gravity is assessed separately through a hydrostatic-to-capillary pressure ratio and does not enter the thermal energy equation. Across repeated five-fold cross-validation, the GP has lower normalised mean absolute error than the neural network for all eight outputs. In 10 held-out boundary cases, 74 of 80 solver responses fall within nominal 90% GP predictive intervals. A three-output GP query takes 249 microseconds, including scaling and predictive variance. In a simulated UR5 L-shaped-seam study, a proportional controller adjusts the synergic power-source setting as travel speed falls near the corner. Finite-volume re-evaluation finds all 12 closed-loop operating points and 9 of 12 open-loop points within an illustrative 1.0-3.0 mm root-side-width band. These simulation results support using the framework to screen welding conditions and control strategies before experimental tests. Thermal-vacuum, targeted reduced-gravity, and robotic hardware-in-the-loop studies are needed to assess the model and controller.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 7 figures. An alternative version was accepted for oral presentation at the 77th International Astronautical Congress (IAC 2026)</p></details> |
| **[TS-WAM: Temporal Latent Actions and World-Action Coupling for Vision-Language Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21922v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many vision-language-action policies rely on action-labeled robot demonstrations and lack an explicit mechanism for predicting future visual states. These limitations hinder learning from action-free videos and can encourage overfitting to demonstration conditions, weakening generalization and robustness. We present TS-WAM, a lightweight Teacher-Student World-Action Model that addresses these challenges through self-supervised temporal learning and explicit future prediction. Its vlm-jepa framework learns from heterogeneous data, including robot demonstrations and action-free human videos, using an EMA target branch without requiring a separately trained external teacher. By coupling vlm-jepa with delta-lwm, TS-WAM learns to predict latent actions from the current observation and instruction and uses them to predict future visual features that guide action generation at inference. A unified end-to-end training architecture jointly optimizes temporal representation learning, visual dynamics, and robot control without a separate latent-action or world-model pretraining stage. The EMA target branch is removed at inference, keeping the deployed policy lightweight. Experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and SimplerEnv, together with real-world manipulation experiments on Franka Research 3 and RevoTron, demonstrate that TS-WAM achieves strong manipulation performance, generalization, and robustness.</p></details> |  |
| **[What Should Robot Policies Learn to Predict? Rethinking Supervision Targets in Vision-Language-Action Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21915v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) policies are typically trained with action prediction as the primary supervision signal, since robots ultimately execute actions. Auxiliary objectives such as latent-action (LA) and future-vision (FV) prediction are often added to improve policy learning, yet they compete with action prediction for the same training budget. We ask whether, under a fixed budget, allocating a substantial share of training samples to such objectives, rather than to further action prediction, can improve policy learning. We build a unified VLA framework that couples a two-stream multimodal diffusion transformer (MMDiT) with a sample-level router, which assigns each training sample to either action prediction or an auxiliary objective and thus makes supervision allocation explicit. Within this framework, we instantiate three LA targets based on LAPA and its DINO-feature variants, and three FV targets based on DINOv3, V-JEPA 2, and Wan2.2 representations. Across LIBERO, CALVIN, and four real-robot tasks, suitable LA and FV supervision outperforms action-only training under the same budget, even when action prediction receives no more than half of the training samples. FV generally outperforms LA, although the strongest LA target remains competitive on CALVIN. Diagnostic analyses suggest that DINO-based LA targets emphasize relative motion, whereas FV targets emphasize absolute position and task identity. We therefore argue that what robot policies learn to predict, and how training is allocated among these targets, should be treated as explicit design choices rather than defaulting to action-dominant training.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 8 figures. Haitao Shen and Hanyu Liu contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[Sub-Millisecond Reflexes for Continuous Physical Human-Robot Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21913v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continuous physical human-robot interaction requires robots to rapidly adapt their control behaviour without interrupting task execution. However, existing ROS 2 control architectures rely on stop-to-switch mechanisms that introduce substantial delays and interrupt execution. We present an open-source, sub-millisecond reflex engine based on an enhanced ROS 2-compatible multimode controller that enables deterministic switching between control modalities and integrates reflex behaviours for mitigating contact hazards and kinematic limit violations. Experiments on a Franka Robotics manipulator demonstrate switching delays below 0.1 ms, more than four orders of magnitude faster than conventional ros2_control, while maintaining continuous operation during safety-critical events. In a multi-stage manipulation task, the proposed framework reduced execution time by 3.5% and peak trajectory jerk by more than threefold. By eliminating runtime middleware overhead, controller transitions are implemented as lock-free pointer operations, enabling reflex-level responsiveness within an open-source robotics framework.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to IEEE, source code available at: https://github.com/RoboRef/franka_reflex_engine</p></details> |
| **[Cross-Embodiment Causal Transfer from Human Videos to Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21798v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scaling robot manipulation models requires large amounts of interaction data, yet collecting diverse real-robot experience remains costly. Human manipulation videos, in contrast, provide abundant and diverse task experience that could potentially benefit robot learning. However, differences in morphology, action spaces, and control mechanisms make human motions unsuitable as direct supervision for robot actions, raising a scientific question: what knowledge in human videos should be transferred across embodiments to robot manipulation? To address this question, we analyze subtask transfer through the lens of causal beams, focusing on which contextual conditions and interaction dependencies should be retained to support local task effects. Based on this perspective, we propose Causal-Beam-Guided Subtask Transfer (CBST). CBST learns manipulation-related representations from human videos and grounds them in task-level semantic variables using robot demonstrations. Within a predefined manipulation model, causal-beam constraints guide contextual information selection by requiring retained assignments to sustain local mechanism outputs while preserving nontrivial responsiveness to retained inputs. The selected semantic information conditions a world-action model, allowing the robot to generate actions from its own observations without requiring human-robot motion correspondence. Experiments on robot manipulation benchmarks show improvements in task success over the corresponding world-action model baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[Sim-to-Real Transfer for Muscle-Actuated Robots via Generalized Actuator Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09487v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tendon drives paired with soft muscle actuation enable faster and safer robots while potentially accelerating skill acquisition. Still, these systems are rarely used in practice due to inherent nonlinearities, friction, and hysteresis, which complicate modeling and control. So far, these challenges have hindered policy transfer from simulation to real systems. To bridge this gap, we propose a sim-to-real pipeline that learns a neural network model of this complex actuation and leverages established rigid body simulation for the arm dynamics and interactions with the environment. Our method, called Generalized Actuator Network (GenAN), enables actuation model identification across a wide range of robots by learning directly from joint position trajectories rather than requiring torque sensors. Using GenAN on PAMY2, a tendon-driven robot powered by pneumatic artificial muscles, we successfully deploy dynamic but precise goal-reaching, ball-in-a-cup, and table tennis policies, trained entirely in simulation. To the best of our knowledge, this result constitutes the first successful sim-to-real transfer for a four-degrees-of-freedom muscle-actuated robot arm.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://webdav.tuebingen.mpg.de/genan/</p></details> |
| **[Robo2Process: Benchmarking and Learning Process-Aware Understanding in Vision-Language Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13040v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) used as robotic critics, reward generators, and failure detectors must understand how manipulation unfolds, not only whether it succeeds. Yet fine-grained process supervision is not readily available in a unified form, and existing evaluations do not systematically diagnose these abilities. We introduce Robo2Process, a trace-grounded framework for scalable construction of process-aware supervision from heterogeneous robotic execution data. It aligns visual observations with source-native signals and annotations, extracts local process units (LPUs), and generates structured QA supervision through task-specific sampling, deterministic label construction, and ambiguity filtering. We instantiate this framework as RoboProcessBench, a diagnostic benchmark with 12 question families spanning \emph{static monitoring} and \emph{dynamic reasoning}, and ProcessData, a corpus of 57,892 QA pairs across four data sources and 260 manipulation tasks, split into ProcessData-SFT and ProcessData-Eval. Evaluation of a broad suite of VLMs reveals uneven process understanding. Supervision from ProcessData-SFT improves \textit{Qwen2.5-VL-7B} and \textit{InternVL-3-8B} on local state, motion, progress, and primitive-aware cues. External evaluation on RoboRewardBench shows Robo2Process enables out-of-distribution process-aware reasoning abilities. Overall, Robo2Process provides a source-aware pipeline, diagnostic benchmark, and learnable substrate for process-aware understanding in robotic manipulation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Asynchronous Indulgent Gathering of Opaque Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10711v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We consider the benchmarking problem of gathering in an $(N,f)$-fault system consisting of $N$ robots, of which at most $f$ may fail at any execution. The {\em traditional} version asks $N-f$ non-faulty robots to gather at a point not known a priori. The recently studied {\em indulgent} version asks $N-f$ non-faulty robots to gather at the location of a faulty one. Indulgent gathering is impossible in a $(2,1)$-crash system in the $LUMI$ model under semi-synchrony, even when infinitely many colors and coordinate axis agreement are available. In this paper, we establish four results for indulgent gathering under asynchrony in $LUMI$, considering a {\em mobility fault} model in which a robot crash only impacts its mobility but not the operation of the light. We first show that indulgent gathering is impossible in a $(2,1)$-mobility system using 2-colored lights, and then give an optimal solution w.r.t. the number of colors using 3-colored lights. We then give two deterministic algorithms for an $(N,f)$-mobility system, $f<N$, $N,f$ unknown, that exhibit a nice time-color trade-off: One with time $O(N)$ using 7-colored lights and another with time $O(\max\{\ell,f\})$ using 26-colored lights, where $\ell< N$ is the number of distinct convex layers of robot positions in the initial configuration. Our algorithms for an $(N,f)$-mobility system are the first to be analysed time complexity, can withstand obstructed visibility (opaque robot model) and asynchronous scheduling. Interestingly, our algorithms also solve traditional gathering.</p></details> | <details><summary>37 pa...</summary><p>37 pages, 26 figures, and 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[SPEAR: Selective Preference Elicitation with Adaptive Rewards for VLM-Guided Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21621v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent Vision-Language Models (VLMs) enable automated preference labeling for robotic manipulation, yet their deployment remains impractical since each query incurs substantial inference cost, while VLMs frequently abstain on visually challenging comparisons. We propose SPEAR, a framework that transforms VLMs into efficient preference oracles via two synergistic mechanisms. First, we design distinguishability-aware query selection to reduce abstention rates by targeting only informative and distinguishable comparisons. Second, we deploy an adaptive reward transition that bootstraps exploration with VLM-synthesized guidance before preference-based refinement. Experiments on the manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that SPEAR achieves 88.9% average success, outperforming baselines by 23.3 percentage points, while significantly reducing abstention and wall-clock training time on contact-rich tasks. SPEAR learns three Mobile ALOHA manipulation tasks fully online on real hardware, demonstrating end-to-end real-world applicability. By addressing query efficiency and exploration challenges, SPEAR establishes VLMs as practical preference oracles for scalable robot learning without manual reward engineering.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 4 figures. Accepted at IROS 2026. Project page: https://zevinwu.github.io/spear-project</p></details> |
| **[An Introduction to Zero-Order Optimization Techniques for Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.22087v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Zero-order optimization techniques are becoming increasingly popular in robotics due to their ability to handle non-differentiable functions and escape local minima. These advantages make them particularly useful for trajectory optimization and policy optimization. In this work, we propose a mathematical tutorial on random search. It offers a simple and unifying perspective for understanding a wide range of algorithms commonly used in robotics. Leveraging this viewpoint, we classify many trajectory optimization methods under a common framework and derive novel competitive RL algorithms.</p></details> |  |
| **[LIBERO-Industry: Benchmarking Robotic Manipulation under Industrial Process Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16782v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Industrial manipulation in flexible manufacturing, e.g., high-mix, low-volume (HMLV) electronics assembly, requires robot policies to adapt to changing products and production conditions. Such settings impose industrial process constraints, including product variants, moving material flows, finite execution windows, and tight geometric tolerances. However, existing evaluation frameworks do not systematically capture these constraints when assessing generalist robot policies. To address this gap, we introduce LIBERO-Industry, a novel benchmark built on the widely used robot learning benchmark LIBERO, that translates the aforementioned industrial constraints into controllable task conditions and measurable success criteria. Specifically, LIBERO-Industry comprises three classic task scenarios: Static-to-Dynamic (S2D) loading, Dynamic-to-Static (D2S) unloading, and Dynamic-to-Dynamic (D2D) transfer. The suites contain 216 tasks in total that systematically vary object geometry, conveyor speed, interaction-window duration, and geometric acceptance region size. Extensive experiments across five generalist robot policies (including vision-language-action models and world-action models) show that performance progressively degrades as industrial constraints are introduced and combined. Under a shared fine-tuning and evaluation protocol, the LIBERO-Industry co-training condition achieved higher observed target-transfer completion than the original-LIBERO condition.</p></details> | <details><summary>38 pa...</summary><p>38 pages, 14 figures, 20 tables, including appendices. Project website: https://IAIE-PhysicalAI.github.io/LIBERO-Industry/</p></details> |
| **[Attribution-Gated Joint Estimation of Intention and Capability for Assist-as-Needed Upper-Limb Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21534v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Assist-as-needed (AAN) control must distinguish between changes in intended motion and changes in effective voluntary torque capability, even though both can produce similar kinematic and interaction responses. A dual-measurand multimodal MHE-MPC architecture is developed as the base framework, in which the intended trajectory and the task-dependent effective voluntary torque capability are estimated online as distinct quantities, and both estimates are used to adapt robotic assistance. Building on this architecture, the ambiguity between the two update channels is formulated as a continuous credit-assignment problem and resolved by an attribution gate. A trajectory-change indicator is obtained from persistent corrective interaction, whereas a capability-change indicator is derived from the residual between normalized muscle activation and dynamics-reconstructed voluntary torque. Nine healthy participants completed factorial target-height-load trials and a pre-post fatigue cup-transport task. Without target-height input, the gated method generated correctly ordered Low-Middle-High references under every load: target changes were preferentially assigned to trajectory adaptation, whereas added load shifted adaptation toward the capability channel.
