This study investigates Physical Recursive Self-Improvement (Physical RSI), in which physical experience guides lasting updates to both a robot's capabilities and its improvement procedure, making subsequent improvement more effective across tasks. Physical RSI faces challenges from noisy observations, continuous dynamics, costly trials, and irreversible consequences. We present an L0-L5 framework that differentiates capability-level from meta-level updates and within-task from across-task gains, reserving L5 for independently verified recursive gain. We study existing methods around harness adaptation and model training, alongside safety and control mechanisms that govern execution and successive updates. Our analysis reveals that, despite substantial progress in self-improvement, the surveyed literature does not yet demonstrate Physical RSI as defined by our framework. We identify research priorities in reliable feedback, causal attribution, safety preservation, and benchmarking, outlining a path toward robots that autonomously improve how they learn while ensuring safety. The GitHub repository for Physical RSI is available at https://github.com/SAIL-Research-Lab/Physical-RSI.</p></details> |  |
| **[EgoMatrix: Physically Faithful Dexterous Robot Demonstrations from Egocentric Human Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18822v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Egocentric human videos offer a scalable alternative to robot teleoperation for collecting dexterous manipulation data. However, two challenges limit their use as reliable training data: accurate hand-object interaction (HOI) reconstruction from monocular RGB and high-quality robot demonstrations, especially visual observations. We present EgoMatrix, an end-to-end data engine that converts monocular egocentric human videos into bimanual dexterous trajectories and paired robot observation videos, with the trajectories directly replayable on real robots. It reconstructs HOI with emphasis on relative geometry and contact rather than exact metric scene recovery, then uses retargeting and reinforcement learning (RL) to reproduce the demonstrated tasks in simulation. We jointly render robot hands and objects from these simulated trajectories to produce physically faithful observations synchronized with state and control records. Rendering the same trajectories in diverse, task-compatible backgrounds expands paired training data while preserving robot-object motion and contact. Across TACO, OakInk2, and self-collected data, EgoMatrix achieves 54.84% end-to-end success in simulation versus 14.52% for the strongest baseline. Reconstruction reaches state-of-the-art performance on most metrics of the two public benchmarks. Qualitative results show more faithful hand-object interactions than prior methods, while feature-space analysis shows closer alignment with real-robot observations than GPT-6 Astra. Our project page is available at https://spikew726.github.io/EgoMatrix/.</p></details> |  |
| **[SEAR: Self-Evolving Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18792v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier language-model agents can now improve robots on their own: they write control code, test it, and keep what works as skills and memories for the next task. We introduce SEAR (Self-Evolving Agentic Robotics), a framework and benchmark built around two questions. Is the agent really learning, or just trying more often? And does what it learns still help on new tasks, in a new simulator, and on a real robot? The framework runs an agent in three modes: it writes code that becomes the policy (code-as-policy), acts live in the control loop (agent-as-policy), or works as a researcher that fine-tunes a vision-language-action model (auto-research). The benchmark adds controls that separate retained experience from repeated attempts, familiar tasks, privileged simulator state, larger budgets, and luck. Across seven frontier language models, 330 simulated and five physical tasks, and over four years of agent runtime, we highlight three findings. First, the learning curve of a robotic agent depends both on the overall time budget and the task difficulty relative to model capability. Harder tasks make longer sessions worthwhile; on easier tasks, re-rolling short attempts does just as well. Second, control code and shared routines transfer across tasks and simulators, while task-specific solutions do not. On a real robot, we observe a performance gap between agent-designed practice and novel human-designed scenes. Third, different models evolve more effectively in different ways. Some do best acting live, others writing code first. And a text-only model keeps pace with a comparable multimodal one: perception helps with error assessment, but agentic reasoning and tool use can matter more. We release the benchmark and codebase to support future research.</p></details> | 16 pages |
| **[EqC: Equilibrium Energy Control for Introspective Lifelong Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18790v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-term robot autonomy depends on more than effective action generation: a policy must recognize when its behavior is no longer sufficiently supported and use assistance to improve. We introduce Equilibrium Energy Control (EqC), a framework for introspective lifelong robot learning built around an explicit conditional energy landscape. EqC casts generative action refinement as equilibrium-seeking optimization, while the geometry of the refinement process reveals whether the generated behavior remains consistent with the learned landscape. This competence signal enables Energy-Gated DAgger, where persistent energy-consistency violations trigger expert takeover and the resulting recovery trajectories are aggregated for subsequent policy updates. EqC integrates naturally into both Diffusion Transformer policies and flow-matching-based vision-language-action (VLA) policies. Across simulation and real-world bimanual manipulation, EqC matches or improves control performance and robustness while enabling timely intervention near the policy's competence boundary. A real-world study further shows how these interventions progressively expand the range of situations handled autonomously, providing a practical path from static generative controllers toward self-improving robot systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[GreenWAM: A World Foundation Model for Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18774v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot needs a world model that predicts how its actions will change the scene and a policy that chooses those actions. Models are usually built and evaluated for only one of these roles, and every robot records its actions in its own format. We ask whether one robot world model can serve both roles across different bodies, and what makes this work. We present GreenWAM, a world foundation model for robotics, together with the recipe that produces it: (1) a shared anatomical action space that describes 20 robot families, from bimanual arms to dexterous humanoids, in common joint coordinates; (2) joint training on these families in visual, action-conditioned and world-action modes; and (3) policies that split an action expert off the generator. As a world model, GreenWAM attains top-tier trajectory, depth and 3D accuracy on WorldArena 1.0, ahead of every world-action model and video generator in our comparison. As a policy, it scores among the top entries of the RoboTwin 2.0 leaderboard, reaches the highest zero-shot LIBERO-plus success among the compared methods and is competitive with dedicated vision-language-action and world-action policies on CALVIN and LIBERO. We further adapt GreenWAM to a real humanoid, where it succeeds more often than policies adapted from $π_{0.5}$ and from Cosmos 3 Edge, and distill it into a single-step policy for real-time control. Controlled experiments yield three findings: robot specialization improves world prediction and control together; an action expert helps when split off at the start of adaptation; and keeping world-model modes during policy adaptation preserves robustness to scene changes.</p></details> | <details><summary>Techn...</summary><p>Technical report. Project page: https://greenvla.github.io/GreenWAM</p></details> |
| **[Fewer Tokens, Better Action: GPT-6 Astra Robot Agents with 14% Higher Success Rate but 65% Fewer Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01939v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language model (VLM) agents can control robots through visual feedback and action primitives, but repeated LLM calls and redundant observations incur substantial token overhead. We introduce PyRUA-Lean, an interactive code-execution framework in which the agent composes robot primitives, including vision-language-action (VLA) policies, into Python cells that check and retry locally and return only the images and state they request. Across 700 simulated task instances from LIBERO-PRO, RoboTwin 2.0, and RoboCasa365, it raises success from 63.1% to 71.7% over tool calling with the same GPT-6 Astra planner and primitives, using 49% fewer LLM calls and 65% fewer input tokens on jointly solved instances. On RoboDojo, without any VLA policy or privileged information, it reaches 51.6% average success rate (Score 58.2), 19.4 points above the top entry of the public leaderboard. Based on the PyRUA-Lean framework, we train PyRUA-Lean Coder v0.1-4B, an open 4B model, with GRPO on 45 LIBERO-PRO instances; it improves from 18.8% to 44.1% on unseen task instances, surpassing the Qwen3.5-27B base model.</p></details> |  |
| **[RoboFQE: Rollout-Free Evaluation of Visual Robot Policies from Offline Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18699v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating robot policies before deployment typically requires producing new trajectories through real-world execution, simulators, or learned world models. These rollout-based approaches impose infrastructure requirements; simulator and world-model evaluators additionally face prediction errors that can compound over the evaluation horizon. In this paper, we study off-policy evaluation as an alternative to rollout-based robot-policy evaluation methods. Specifically, we introduce RoboFQE, an evaluator that estimates the discounted expected return of a target policy from offline robot trajectories without estimating a behavior-policy density, combining fitted Q-evaluation with a pretrained vision foundation model and learned terminal success annotation to bridge the gap between standard OPE benchmarks and continuous-control visual manipulation. Controlled experiments across simulated and real-world manipulation tasks show that RoboFQE produces accurate discounted-return estimates and preserves policy rankings even on long-horizon manipulation tasks where video world-model evaluators are degraded by autoregressive prediction errors. Ablation experiments show that pretrained vision encoders substantially outperform from-scratch alternatives, particularly on long-horizon tasks, and that learned terminal success annotation enables evaluation without ground-truth reward labels with only slight reduction of accuracy.</p></details> |  |
| **[Uncertainty-Aware Action Segmentation for Human-to-Robot Assembly Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08341v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Action segmentation supports intention estimation and execution monitoring in assisted teleoperation, but robot demonstrations are costly to collect. We propose an uncertainty-aware framework that transfers temporal knowledge from human to robot demonstrations through MS-TCN++~\cite{li2020mstcnpp} pretraining and fine-tuning, reliability-aware sequence correction, and class-conditional conformal prediction. Across 22 toy-car assembly action classes, fine-tuning with 9 robot demonstrations improves test Edit score from 55.23 to 72.51 and segmental F1@50 from 10.97 to 16.86. The reliability analyzer identifies incorrect segments with 0.755 AUROC. Unrestricted correction reduces Edit to 67.86, while selective acceptance maintains 73.45, primarily safeguarding against harmful relabeling. Conformal prediction achieves 95.38\% empirical coverage at a 93\% target, but prediction sets average 17.41 of 22 classes. These findings support human temporal pretraining while revealing persistent challenges in reliable correction and informative uncertainty quantification with limited robot supervision.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 6 figures. Preprint version</p></details> |
| **[Perception Beyond the Visible: Incompleteness-Aware Scene Understanding for Robot Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18670v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable robot autonomy requires reasoning about the world from partial and noisy observations. However, many perception systems model only visible scene regions, rely on fixed semantic categories, and assume reliable sensor measurements. These assumptions break under occlusion, novel object encounters, and sensor degradation. In this paper, I present a research vision for incompleteness-aware scene understanding. I organize this vision around three fundamental challenges: incomplete evidence, incomplete knowledge, and incomplete sensor reliability. I discuss my research contributions toward addressing these challenges through amodal perception, semantic occupancy forecasting, open-world scene understanding, label-efficient learning, and uncertainty-guided multi-modal fusion. Building on these contributions, I outline future research directions toward belief-centric 4D scene representations. These representations aim to jointly model geometry, semantics, motion, and uncertainty. I also discuss active sensing and safe adaptation strategies that allow robots to reduce uncertainty through interaction. The goal is to develop calibrated and actionable scene representations that support reliable autonomy in dynamic, open-world environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Resea...</summary><p>Research statement for RSS Pioneers 2026 (Robotics: Science and Systems). OpenReview: https://openreview.net/forum?id=wP5JjTHXnQ</p></details> |
| **[From Perception to Symbolic Task Planning: Vision-Language Guided Human-Robot Collaborative Structured Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.00978v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human-robot collaboration (HRC) in structured assembly requires reliable state estimation and adaptive task planning under noisy perception and human interventions. To address these challenges, we introduce a design-grounded human-aware planning framework for human-robot collaborative structured assembly. The framework comprises two coupled modules. Module I, Perception-to-Symbolic State (PSS), employs vision-language models (VLMs) based agents to align RGB-D observations with design specifications and domain knowledge, synthesizing verifiable symbolic assembly states. It outputs validated installed and uninstalled component sets for online state tracking. Module II, Human-Aware Planning and Replanning (HPR), performs task-level multi-robot assignment and updates the plan only when the observed state deviates from the expected execution outcome. It applies a minimal-change replanning rule to selectively revise task assignments and preserve plan stability even under human interventions. We validate the framework on a 27-component timber-frame assembly. The PSS module achieves 97% state synthesis accuracy, and the HPR module maintains feasible task progression across diverse HRC scenarios. Results indicate that integrating VLM-based perception with knowledge-driven planning improves robustness of state estimation and task planning under dynamic conditions.</p></details> |  |
| **[All-Electric Heavy-Duty Robotic Manipulator: Surrogate-Enhanced Modeling and Adaptive Modular Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06313v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a unified system-level modeling and control framework for an all-electric heavy-duty robotic manipulator (HDRM) driven by electromechanical linear actuators (EMLAs). A surrogate-enhanced actuator model, combining integrated electromechanical dynamics with a neural network trained on a dedicated testbed, is integrated into an extended virtual decomposition control (VDC) architecture augmented by a natural adaptation law. The derived analytical HDRM model supports a hierarchical control structure that seamlessly maps high-level force and velocity objectives to real-time actuator commands, accompanied by a Lyapunov-based stability proof. In multi-domain simulations of both cubic and a custom planar triangular trajectory, the proposed adaptive modular controller achieves sub-centimeter Cartesian tracking accuracy. Experimental validation of the same 1-DoF platform under realistic load emulation confirms the efficacy of the proposed control strategy. These findings demonstrate that a surrogate-enhanced EMLA model embedded in the VDC approach can enable modular, real-time control of an all-electric HDRM, supporting its deployment in next-generation mobile working machines.</p></details> | <details><summary>Revis...</summary><p>Revised version of arXiv:2508.06313. This is the same work with an updated title, previously titled: Surrogate-Enhanced Modeling and Adaptive Modular Control of All-Electric Heavy-Duty Robotic Manipulators.
